, illustrating that non-Gaussianity does not appear to affect the convergence. However the bias for the standardized estimates under the equi-correlated covariance structure is greater.

8.2
Influence of weighting

Figure
A3
compares
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm GWASH}
and
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm LDSC}
with their weighted versions
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm GWASH-W}
and
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm LDSC-W}
, as defined in (
20
) and (
18
), for both standardized and unstandardized data. The weighting schemes are designed to deal with non-stationarities that can arise in practice
(
Bulik-Sullivan et al., 2015
;
Pham et al., 2026
)
. Thus, we simulate data with a mixed, non-stationary correlation structure in which the first and second set of
m/2
markers are generated from a AR(1) process with correlations
\rho=0.2
and
0.9
, respectively.

Comparing the different weighting schemes without standardizing, the SE and bias of the different estimators decrease with
n
for all estimators. Without standardization, GWASH and LDSC are more distinguishable and the effect of weighting is less pronounced compared to the standardized data.

For the standardized data, the weighted versions of LDSC and GWASH have the lowest SEs, highlighting the value of incorporating weighting schemes when the dependence structure varies. The two estimators have similar SEs and give similar results in practice, reflecting the equivalence discussed in Section
5.1
.
We note that the weighted estimators are slightly more biased than the unweighted estimators leading to a bias-variance trade-off. However
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm GWASH}
and
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm GWASH-W}
are slightly less biased than
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm LDSC}
and
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm LDSC-W}
in this setting. As in previous sections, standardizing the data leads to a decrease in the SE of the estimator.

8.3
Bias caused by population stratification

In Section
7
it was claimed that when population stratification is present, both GWASH and LDSC with fixed and free intercept are biased. Furthermore, the asymptotic bias was explicitly computed. We now demonstrate this theoretical result.
We simulated data from Model (
27
) with the parameters
\sigma_{\xi}=0.3
,
h^{2}=0.2
and where
\vec{\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{i}
, defined in Section
7.1
, follows an AR(1) model with parameter
\rho=0.3
. Also, we simulated
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\sim N(0,{\rm Var}(f))
, where
{\rm Var}(f)\in\{0,0.01,0.02,\dots,0.1,0.15,0.2,\dots,0.5\}
.

Figure
A4
plots the simulation bias for
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm GWASH}
,
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm LDSC}
and
\hat{h}^{2}_{\text{LDSC-free}}
, for
n=500
,
m=1000
. For comparison we plot the theoretical (asymptotic) bias for LDSC with the free intercept, as calculated by Theorem
9
for unstandardized data. For larger values of
\text{var}(f)
there is a close match between the theoretical and the bias obtained using each of the estimators. As discussed after Theorem
9
, the bias explodes for low
{\rm Var}(f)
and there is a discontinuity point at
{\rm Var}(f)=0
. However, for low
{\rm Var}(f)
the asymptotic bias comes into play only for large
m
and hence the asymptotic and simulated bias differ. Importantly, Figure
A4
demonstrates that the bias of the estimators can be very extreme and hence when population stratification is present none of these estimators should be used.

9
Discussion

We conclude the paper with a summary, discussion on implications for GWAS studies and possible extensions.

9.1
Summary

The focus of this work has been on heritability estimation and asymptotic properties of the LDSC and related estimators under generic assumptions. Two definitions of heritability were given depending on whether the genetic effects are modeled as fixed or random. It was shown that the two definitions coincide asymptotically when the two conditions of weak dependence (WD) and bounded-kurtosis effects (BKE) hold. These two conditions played an important role in all the theoretical results presented. It was shown that, with some variations, WD and BKE are sufficient conditions for consistency of both the basic LDSC and GWASH estimators, for both Gaussian and non-Gaussian predictors. For Gaussian predictors it was shown that these conditions are also necessary for consistency of GWASH (with truncation). Our simulations suggest that necessity holds too when the predictors are non-Gaussian.

Two modifications are done in practice: weighting and standardization. It was shown that, with properly truncated weights, weighting does not change the consistency results.
However, standardization of the predictors and outcome, as done in practice, introduces asymptotic bias in both LDSC and GWASH if either of the two essential conditions are violated. In the theory sections we did not study the implications of the two modifications together for brevity. However, the simulations indicate that the practical estimators, in which both modifications are applied, are well-behaved under the essential conditions and exhibit bias otherwise.

When population stratification is present, we have shown that both fixed intercept LDSC and GWASH are biased. To deal with population stratification,
Bulik-Sullivan et al. (2015)
suggested using the LDSC regression estimator with free intercept. However, as shown in theory in Theorem
9
and in practice in Section
8.3
, the free intercept estimator does not eliminate the bias in this scenario. Hence, we recommend that, when population stratification is present, neither LDSC nor GWASH should be used.

9.2
Implications for GWAS studies

The LDSC regression estimator is widely used in GWAS (as of May 2026 there are over 6000 citations of
Bulik-Sullivan et al. (2015)
in Google Scholar). Our findings have significant implications for its practical application. We have found that when the two above essential conditions are violated, LDSC is inconsistent. In particular, if high correlation or strong effects are present in the data, LDSC has high variance. When the data is standardized, as done in practice, LDSC is also biased if the two conditions are violated. Finally, under the common problem of population stratification, LDSC, even with free intercept, is highly biased and inconsistent. These findings allow the user to inspect the data and evaluate whether LDSC is useful for the data at hand.

The title of
Bulik-Sullivan et al. (2015)
is “LD Score regression distinguishes confounding from polygenicity in genome-wide association studies”. Thus, the main claim in that paper is that
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm LDSC-free}
can account for population stratification, a source of confounding in their terminology. However, one important finding of our work is that this is not the case, but rather
\hat{h}^{2}_{\rm LDSC-free}
is biased under population stratification. As mentioned above, this finding has significant practical implications.

Because of the scope of this problem, this paper focuses on its theoretical aspects. In a companion article
(
Pham et al., 2026
)
, we consider realistic simulations and examples. In particular,
Pham et al. (2026)
demonstrates, using realistic simulations and data, that LDSC with constraint intercept has smaller variance than the free-intercept version, which is widely used. It is also demonstrated that when population stratification is present, the estimators are biased.

As mentioned above, the two essential conditions for consistency are weak dependence and BKE. Section 8.4 in
Schwartzman et al. (2019)
demonstrates how the weak dependence condition can be verified in GWAS data. In particular,
\hat{\mu}_{2}
is estimated in sub-samples with varying size, and its limiting behavior as
m\to\infty
can be analyzed. Checking the BKE condition in real data is discussed in Section 5 of
Nan et al. (2026)
.

To correct the bias caused by population stratification, it was suggested in several papers (see e.g.,
Yang et al. (2011)
) to regress out the first few principal components of
{\bf S}
. This eliminates the
\boldsymbol{\xi}
term that appears under the population stratification model (
27
), and makes
{{\boldsymbol{X}}}
weakly dependent, allowing for unbiased and consistent estimation as long as the BKE condition holds. However, this requires access to the original data, that is, not just summary statistics data.
To correct the bias caused by violations of the BKE condition,
Nan et al. (2026)
suggests an FVE decomposition using GWASH to handle such cases, but this method also requires access to the original data.

9.3
Extensions

Extensions of the results presented here can go in several directions. First, the focus here was on consistency and asymptotic bias and not on inference. In practice, construction of confidence intervals and performance of hypothesis testing is essential. The study of those issues under realistic scenarios is done in
Pham et al. (2026)
.
A theoretical extension in this direction would be to establish CLTs for the estimators.

Second, it would be interesting to consider heritability estimation in extended settings like in the presence of other covariates
(
Luo et al., 2021
)
and partitioning of heritability by functional annotations
(
Finucane et al., 2015
)
.

Third,
Nan et al. (2026)
has shown in simulations that GCTA also gives biased results under violations of the BKE condition. Further theoretical analysis is needed to study the performance of GCTA and other methods such as PCGC under violations of BKE and weak dependence.

Finally, it would be interesting to study these FVE estimators in application to other domains beyond genetics, which may bring their own challenges and opportunities. For example, neuroimaging data has a spatial structure and can exhibit strong dependence
(
Azriel and Schwartzman, 2020
)
, but in general the original data is observed with no need to reduce to summary statistics. Yet, estimators based on summary statistics may still be useful. This is a topic for future research.

Appendix A
Further simulation materials

A.1
Generation of binomial variables with AR(1) correlation

The following procedure generates vectors
\vec{x}_{i}
that follow an AR(1) correlation structure with parameter
\rho
, and where marginally
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}\sim\text{Binomial}(2,p)
:

1.

Initialization:
Let
k=1
,
\chi^{(k)}_{1}\sim\text{Bernoulli}(p)
and define
p_{1}:=p+\rho(1-p)
and
p_{0}:=(1-p_{1})p/(1-p)
.

2.

Randomize
\chi^{(k)}_{j}
given
\chi^{(k)}_{j-1}
:
For
j=2,\ldots,m
,

(a)

Let
p_{j}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}p_{1}&\text{ if }\chi^{(k)}_{j-1}=1\\
p_{0}&\text{ if }\chi^{(k)}_{j-1}=0\end{array}\right.
.

(b)

Randomize
\chi_{j}^{(k)}\sim Bernoulli(p_{j})
.

3.

Output
: Repeat steps 1 and 2 for
k=2
and define
\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}=(\chi^{(1)}_{1}+\chi^{(2)}_{1},\ldots,\chi^{(1)}_{m}+\chi^{(2)}_{m})
.

Appendix B
Further theoretical results

B.1
Sufficient conditions for the weighted estimators

To analyze the effect of weighting and allow for different weighting schemes, let
g(a,b)
be any differentiable function satisfying

\left|\frac{\partial g(a,b)}{\partial a}\right|\leq Cb^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left|\frac{\partial g(a,b)}{\partial b}\right|\leq Cb,

(30)

for some constant
C
. In particular, the weights (
19
) correspond to
w_{j}=g\left(\hat{h}^{2},\mathaccentV{bar}016{\ell}_{j,R}\right)
with

g(a,b)=\left(1+\frac{n}{m}ab\right)^{-2}b^{-1},\qquad 0\leq a\leq 1,\qquad b\geq 1.

This function
g
satisfies restriction (
30
) because

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial g(a,b)}{\partial a}\right|\leq\frac{\frac{2n}{m}}{\left(1+\frac{n}{m}ab\right)^{3}}+\frac{1}{\left(1+\frac{n}{m}ab\right)^{2}b^{2}}\leq\frac{2n}{m}+1\qquad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial g(a,b)}{\partial b}\right|=\frac{\frac{2an}{m}}{\left(1+\frac{n}{m}ab\right)^{3}b}\leq\frac{2n}{m}.

As before, consistency is achieved by proving consistency in
{\cal L}_{2}
of both the numerator and denominator separately. The following result is a modification of Theorems
4
and
5
in the weighted case, considering both Gaussian and non-Gaussian predictors.

Theorem 10
.

Consider Model (
1
) and assume that
m/n\to\lambda>0
. Let
\hat{h}^{2}
be any consistent estimator of
h^{2}
, i.e. satisfying
\hat{h}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}h^{2}
as
m,n\to\infty
. Suppose also that

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}g\left(h^{2},\underline{\ell}_{j}\right)\underline{\ell}_{j}^{2}

converges to a finite limit as
m\to\infty
.

1.

(Gaussian case) If
\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}\sim N({\boldsymbol{0}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
, and BKE and WD
2
hold, then
\hat{h}_{\rm LDSC-W}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}h^{2}
as
m,n\to\infty
.

2.

(Non-Gaussian case) If BKE, M
2
and WD
2
hold, then
\hat{h}_{\rm LDSC-W}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}h^{2}
as
m,n\to\infty
.

By the argument made in Section
5.2
, the weighted GWASH estimator (
20
) is almost equivalent to the weighted LDSC estimator (
18
). Therefore, by a similar argument to Theorem
10
, it can be shown that the weighted GWASH estimator (
20
) is consistent under similar conditions. The finite-sample performance of this estimator is studied in
Pham et al. (2026)
.

Appendix C
Proofs

C.1
Proof of Theorem
1

Under the random-effects model,
h^{2}_{\rm\beta}=\frac{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+1-h^{2}}
. The function
f(u):=\frac{u}{u+1-h^{2}}
is continuous and invertible for
u\geq 0
and
f(h^{2})=h^{2}
. Therefore, to prove the result it is enough to show that
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
converges to
h^{2}
in
{\cal L}_{2}
iff conditions BKE and WD
0
hold. This is shown next.

By the law of total expectation,
{\rm E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right)=h^{2}
. Therefore,
{\rm E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-h^{2}\right)^{2}={\rm Var}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right)
. The variance of the quadratic form
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is
(
Petersen and Pedersen, 2008
, Eq.(319))

{\rm Var}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right)=2[{\rm E}(\beta_{i}^{2})]^{2}{\rm tr}({{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2})+[{\rm E}(\beta_{i}^{4})-3[{\rm E}(\beta_{i}^{2})]^{2}]m=2h^{4}\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}({{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2})+h^{4}\frac{1}{m}[{\rm Kurt}(\beta_{i})-3].

Therefore,
{\rm E}_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-h^{2}\right)^{2}\to 0
iff
\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}({{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2})\to 0
and
\frac{1}{m}[{\rm Kurt}(\beta_{i})-3]\to 0
.
∎

C.2
The GWASH estimator of
Schwartzman et al. (2019)
vs. Definition (
9
)

In Eq. (19) of
Schwartzman et al. (2019)
, the GWASH estimator has the same form as (
9
), except that the estimator of the second spectral moment in Eq. (21) of
Schwartzman et al. (2019)
, written in our notation, is

\hat{\mu}_{2}=\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(\boldsymbol{S}^{2}_{R})-\frac{m-1}{n-1}=\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(\boldsymbol{S}^{2}_{R})-\frac{m}{n}+O(1/n).

This estimator differs from the estimator in (
11
) by
O(1/n)
. Since the numerators of the GWASH estimators in Eq. (19) of
Schwartzman et al. (2019)
and (
9
) are the same, we conclude that the two estimators differ by
O(1/n)
.

C.3
Proof of Proposition
1

For the proof, we need two lemmas.

Lemma 1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}
and
\boldsymbol{B}
two fixed symmetric matrices of dimension
d\times d
, and let
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})^{T}
be a vector of iid random variables with mean zero and finite fourth moment. Let
{\cal M}_{2}
and
{\cal M}_{4}
denote the second and fourth moment of
\xi_{i}
. Then,
{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{\xi}})=({\rm diag(\boldsymbol{A})})^{T}{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{B})({\cal M}_{4}-3{\cal M}_{2}^{2})+2{\rm tr}(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B}){\cal M}_{2}^{2}
.

Proof of Lemma
1
:

We have that

{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{\xi}})=\sum_{i,i^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}}{\rm Cov}(A_{ii^{\prime}}\xi_{i}\xi_{i^{\prime}},B_{hh^{\prime}}\xi_{h}\xi_{h^{\prime}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}A_{ii}B_{ii}{\rm Var}(\xi_{i}^{2})&i=i^{\prime}=h=h^{\prime}\\
A_{ii^{\prime}}B_{ii^{\prime}}{\cal M}_{2}^{2}&i=h\neq h^{\prime}=i^{\prime}\\
A_{ii^{\prime}}B_{ii^{\prime}}{\cal M}_{2}^{2}&i=h^{\prime}\neq h=i^{\prime}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right..

Since
{\rm Var}(\xi_{i}^{2})={\cal M}_{4}-{\cal M}_{2}^{2}
,

{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\xi}},{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{\xi}})=\sum_{i}A_{ii}B_{ii}({\cal M}_{4}-{\cal M}_{2}^{2})+2\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}A_{ii^{\prime}}B_{ii^{\prime}}{\cal M}_{2}^{2}\\
=\sum_{i}A_{ii}B_{ii}({\cal M}_{4}-3{\cal M}_{2}^{2})+2\sum_{i,i^{\prime}}A_{ii^{\prime}}B_{ii^{\prime}}{\cal M}_{2}^{2}=({\rm diag(\boldsymbol{A})})^{T}{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{B})({\cal M}_{4}-3{\cal M}_{2}^{2})+2{\rm tr}(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B}){\cal M}_{2}^{2}.

∎

Lemma 2
.

Under Model (
1
),

\displaystyle{\rm Cov}(u_{j}^{2},u_{k}^{2}|{\boldsymbol{X}})=

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}^{2})^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{2}\frac{h^{4}n^{2}}{m^{2}}[{\rm Kurt}(\beta_{i})-3]+\frac{1}{n^{2}}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2})^{T}{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{k}^{2}(1-h^{2})^{2}[{\rm Kurt}(\varepsilon_{i})-3]

\displaystyle+2\left(\frac{n}{m}\boldsymbol{S}^{2}_{j,k}h^{2}+(1-h^{2})S_{j,k}\right)^{2},

where
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}:=\frac{1}{n}{\bf X}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}
, and
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}^{2},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2}
are the element-wise square of
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
.

Proof of Lemma
2
:

We have that

\displaystyle{\rm Cov}(u_{j}^{2},u_{k}^{2}|{\boldsymbol{X}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+2{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+2{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|{\boldsymbol{X}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|{\boldsymbol{X}})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n^{2}}{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|{\boldsymbol{X}})

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{n^{2}}{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|{\boldsymbol{X}}).

By Lemma
1
, the first two terms are

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|{\boldsymbol{X}})

\displaystyle=n^{2}({\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}^{2})^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{2}[{\rm E}(\beta_{i}^{4})-3h^{4}/m^{2}]+2n^{2}({\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k})^{2}h^{4}/m^{2}

\displaystyle=({\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}^{2})^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{2}\frac{h^{4}n^{2}}{m^{2}}[{\rm Kurt}(\beta_{i})-3]+2n^{2}({\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k})^{2}h^{4}/m^{2}

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|{\boldsymbol{X}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2})^{T}{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{k}^{2}[{\rm E}(\varepsilon_{i}^{4})-3(1-h^{2})^{2}]+\frac{2}{n^{2}}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k})^{2}(1-h^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2})^{T}{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{k}^{2}(1-h^{2})^{2}[{\rm Kurt}(\varepsilon_{i})-3]+\frac{2}{n^{2}}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k})^{2}(1-h^{2})^{2}.

The last covariance to compute is

\frac{4}{n^{2}}{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|{\boldsymbol{X}})=4{\rm E}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|{\boldsymbol{X}})\\
=4(1-h^{2}){\rm E}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}|{\boldsymbol{X}})=4(1-h^{2})\frac{h^{2}}{m}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{T})\\
=4\frac{(1-h^{2})h^{2}}{m}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}.

Collecting all the terms and noticing that

\frac{n}{m}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}h^{2}+(1-h^{2})\frac{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}}{n}=\frac{n}{m}\boldsymbol{S}^{2}_{j,k}h^{2}+(1-h^{2})S_{j,k}

implies the result of Lemma
2
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1
:
.
Since
N_{\rm GWASH}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(u_{j}^{2}-1\right)
, Lemma
2
implies that,

{\rm Var}\left(N_{\rm GWASH}|{\boldsymbol{X}}\right)=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}{\rm Cov}(u_{j}^{2},u_{k}^{2}|{\boldsymbol{X}})=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}({\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}^{2})^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{2}\frac{h^{4}n^{2}}{m^{2}}[{\rm Kurt}(\beta_{i})-3]\\
+\frac{(1-h^{2})^{2}[{\rm Kurt}(\varepsilon_{i})-3]}{n^{2}m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2})^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{2}+\frac{2}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}\left(\frac{n}{m}h^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{2}_{j,k}+(1-h^{2})\boldsymbol{S}_{j,k}\right)^{2}.

Now,

\sum_{j,k}({\boldsymbol{s}}_{j}^{2})^{T}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{2}=\sum_{j,k,p}{\boldsymbol{S}}^{2}_{j,p}{\boldsymbol{S}}^{2}_{k,p}=\sum_{p=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\boldsymbol{S}}^{2}_{j,p}\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\boldsymbol{S}}^{2}_{k,p}=\sum_{p=1}^{m}\hat{\ell}_{p}^{2},

and

\sum_{j,k}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2})^{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,k}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}\|^{4}.

Also,

\sum_{j,k}\left(\frac{n}{m}h^{2}\boldsymbol{S}^{2}_{j,k}+(1-h^{2})\boldsymbol{S}_{j,k}\right)^{2}={\rm tr}\left(h^{2}\frac{n}{m}\boldsymbol{S}^{2}+(1-h^{2})\boldsymbol{S}\right)^{2},

which completes the proof of the proposition. ∎

C.4
Proof of Theorem
2

Proof of Part (i):

Recall that
D_{\rm GWASH}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{n}{m}\hat{\ell}_{j,R}
. By (
5
),
{\rm E}(\hat{\ell}_{j,R})=\ell_{j}(1+1/n)
. Therefore,

{\rm E}(D_{\rm GWASH})=\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\right)\frac{n}{m}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ell_{j}\to\frac{\mu_{2}}{\lambda}.

Thus, in order to show convergence in
{\cal L}_{2}
it is enough to show that
{\rm Var}(D_{\rm GWASH})\to 0
.

We have that

{\rm Var}(D_{\rm GWASH})=\frac{n^{2}}{m^{2}}{\rm Var}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\ell}_{j}\right).

The factor
n^{2}/m^{2}
converges to a constant, and
by Lemma
3
below we have that

{\rm Var}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\ell}_{j}\right)\leq\frac{8{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})+\frac{8}{n}\left[{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right]^{2}+16\frac{m}{n}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{3})}{nm^{2}}+O(1/n),

which converges to zero under WD
1
. Notice that
{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{3})\leq m{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})=O(m^{2})
under WD
1
. Thus, the result follows.

Proof of Part (ii):

Recall that
N_{\rm GWASH}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(u_{j}^{2}-1)
and by (
6
),

{\rm E}(u_{j}^{2}|{\boldsymbol{X}})=h^{2}\left(\frac{n}{m}\hat{\ell}_{j}-d_{j}^{2}\right)+d_{j}^{2}
.
Therefore,

{\rm E}(N_{\rm GWASH})={\rm E}[(N_{\rm GWASH}|{\boldsymbol{X}})]\\
=h^{2}\frac{n}{m}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\rm E}\left(\hat{\ell}_{j}-\frac{m}{n}d_{j}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\rm E}(d_{j}^{2}-1)=h^{2}\frac{n}{m}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\rm E}\left(\hat{\ell}_{j}-\frac{m}{n}\right)

because
E(d_{j}^{2})=1
. And by the computation in Part (i) we have that
{\rm E}(N_{\rm GWASH})\to h^{2}\mu_{2}/\lambda
.

Consider now
{\rm Var}(N_{\rm GWASH})
. We have the variance decomposition

{\rm Var}(N_{\rm GWASH})={\rm Var}(E(N_{\rm GWASH}|{\boldsymbol{X}}))+{\rm E}({\rm Var}(N_{\rm GWASH}|{\boldsymbol{X}})).

By similar arguments as in Part (i),
{\rm Var}(E(N_{\rm GWASH}|{\boldsymbol{X}}))\to 0
under WD
1
. We now compute the second term. By (
12
),

{\rm E}({\rm Var}(N_{\rm GWASH}|{\boldsymbol{X}}))=\frac{1}{m}\left[{\rm Kurt}(\beta_{i})-3\right]\frac{h^{4}n^{2}}{m^{2}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\rm E}\left(\hat{\ell}_{j}^{2}\right)\\
+\left[{\rm Kurt}(\varepsilon_{i})-3\right]\frac{(1-h^{2})^{2}}{n}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\rm E}\left(\frac{\|\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}\|^{2}}{m}\right)^{2}+\frac{2}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}\left[{\rm E}\left(h^{2}\frac{n}{m}\boldsymbol{S}^{2}+(1-h^{2})\boldsymbol{S}\right)^{2}\right].

(31)

We have that

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\rm E}(\hat{\ell}_{j}^{2})=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}[{\rm E}(\hat{\ell}_{j})]^{2}+\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\rm Var}(\hat{\ell}_{j})=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left[\ell_{j}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\right)+\frac{m}{n}\right]^{2}+O(1),

where in the last equality we used
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\rm Var}(\hat{\ell}_{j})=O(1)
, which follows from (
34
) below because it implies that

{\rm Var}(\hat{\ell}_{j})=\frac{4}{n}\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}\\
+\frac{8}{n^{2}}\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2})+O(1/n)\\
\leq\frac{4}{n}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}+\ell_{j}^{2})+\frac{2}{n^{2}}({\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})+\ell_{j}^{2})+\frac{8}{n^{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}+m\ell_{j})+O(1/n).

(32)

Notice that
\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}={\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{3})\leq m{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})=O(m^{2})
. Also, under WD
1
,
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ell_{j}^{2}
is bounded. It follows that
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\rm E}(\hat{\ell}_{j}^{2})=O(1)
.

Furthermore,

{\rm E}(\|\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}\|^{4})=\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}}{\rm E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i{j_{1}}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i{j_{2}}}^{2}]=O(m^{2}),

and hence
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\rm E}\left(\frac{\|\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}\|^{2}}{m}\right)^{2}=O(1)
.
Finally, by the equations given on page 99 of
Gupta and Nagar (2018)
we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm E}\left[{\rm tr}(\boldsymbol{S}^{2})\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\mu_{2}+\frac{1}{m}\mu_{1}^{2}+O(1/n^{2}),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm E}\left[{\rm tr}(\boldsymbol{S}^{3})\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\mu_{3}+3\frac{1}{m}\mu_{2}\mu_{1}+\frac{m}{n^{2}}\mu_{1}^{3}+O(1/n^{2}),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm E}\left[{\rm tr}(\boldsymbol{S}^{4})\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\mu_{4}+4\frac{1}{n}\mu_{3}\mu_{1}+2\frac{1}{n}\mu_{2}^{2}+6\frac{m}{n^{2}}\mu_{2}\mu_{1}^{2}+\frac{m^{2}}{n^{3}}\mu_{1}^{4}+O(1/n^{2}),

where
\mu_{k}:={\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{k})/m
,
k=1,\ldots,4
. Notice that if
\mu_{4}/m
converges to zero, which we assumed under WD
1
, then so does
\mu_{3}/m
. This is because

\frac{1}{m}\mu_{k}=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{j}^{k}=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}\leq 1}\lambda_{j}^{k}+\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j:\lambda_{j}>1}\lambda_{j}^{k},

(33)

where
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}
are the eigenvalues of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
. The first summand converges to zero as it is bounded by
1/m
, and the second summand is larger for
k=4
than for
k=3
.

It follows that

{\rm E}({\rm Var}(N_{\rm GWASH}|{\boldsymbol{X}}))=\frac{1}{m}\left[{\rm Kurt}(\beta_{i})-3\right]O(1)+o(1),

which converges to zero due to the BKE assumption. This completes the proof of the theorem. ∎

Lemma 3
.

Suppose that
\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1},\ldots,\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{n}
are iid
N({\boldsymbol{0}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
and that
m/n
is fixed then

{\rm Var}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\ell}_{j}\right)\leq\frac{8{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})+\frac{8}{n}\left[{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right]^{2}+16\frac{m}{n}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{3})}{nm^{2}}+O(1/n).

Proof of Lemma
3
:

Let us compute

{\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}})^{2},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})^{2}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{3},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{3},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{2}}).

Consider the following cases in which the covariance is not zero:

1.

•

i_{1}=i_{3}\neq i_{2}\neq i_{4}
(i.e.,
i_{4}
is also different from
i_{1}
): there are
n(n-1)(n-2)
such cases and then

{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{2}})\\
={\rm E}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}){\rm E}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}}){\rm E}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{2}})\\
-{\rm E}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}})E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}})E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{3},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{3},p_{2}})E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{2}})\\
=({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}^{2}\\
=({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}

•

There are three other symmetric cases:
i_{1}=i_{4}\neq i_{2}\neq i_{3}
,
i_{1}\neq i_{2}=i_{3}\neq i_{4}
,
i_{1}\neq i_{2}=i_{4}\neq i_{3}
. Therefore, there are
4n(n-1)(n-2)
such cases.

2.

•

i_{1}=i_{3}\neq i_{2}=i_{4}
: there are
n(n-1)
such cases and then

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{2}})

\displaystyle=\{{\rm E}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}})\}^{2}

\displaystyle-E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}})E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}})E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}})E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{2}})

\displaystyle-E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}})E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}})E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}})E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{2}})

\displaystyle=({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}+2({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}

•

The case
i_{1}=i_{4}\neq i_{2}=i_{3}
is symmetric. Therefore, there are
2n(n-1)
such cases.

3.

•

i_{1}=i_{2}=i_{3}\neq i_{4}
: there are
n(n-1)
such cases and then

{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}^{2},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{2}})\\
=\left[{\rm E}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}})-E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}^{2})E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}})\right]E({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{2}})\\
=\left[{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+2{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+2{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}+2({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+4{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}\right.\\
+\left.4{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}-(1+2({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}\right]{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}\\
=\left[2{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+2{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}+4{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+4{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}\right]{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}

•

There are three other symmetric cases:
i_{1}\neq i_{2}=i_{3}=i_{4}
,
i_{1}=i_{3}=i_{4}\neq i_{2}
,
i_{1}=i_{2}=i_{4}\neq i_{3}
. Therefore, there are
4n(n-1)
such cases.

4.

For the case
i_{1}=i_{2}=i_{3}=i_{4}
we just bound the covariance by a constant.

It follows that

{\rm Cov}((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}})^{2},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})^{2})=\frac{4+O(1/n)}{n}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}\\
+\frac{2+O(1/n)}{n^{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}\\
+\frac{8+O(1/n)}{n^{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})+O(1/n^{3}).

Therefore,

{\rm Cov}(\hat{\ell}_{j},\hat{\ell}_{k})={\rm Cov}\left(\sum_{p}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p})^{2},\sum_{p}(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p})^{2}\right)=\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\rm Cov}((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}})^{2},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})^{2})\\
=\frac{4}{n}\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}\\
+\frac{8}{n^{2}}\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})+O(1/n)

(34)

and

{\rm Var}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\ell}_{j}\right)=\frac{\sum_{j,p_{1},k,p_{2}}{\rm Cov}[(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}})^{2},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})^{2}]}{m^{2}}\\
=\frac{4}{nm^{2}}\sum_{j,p_{1},k,p_{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+\frac{2}{n^{2}m^{2}}\sum_{j,p_{1},k,p_{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}\\
+\frac{8}{n^{2}m^{2}}\sum_{j,p_{1},k,p_{2}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})+O(1/n)\\
\leq\frac{8{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})+\frac{8}{n}\left[{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right]^{2}+16\frac{m}{n}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{3})}{nm^{2}}+O(1/n).

where the inequality in the last line is due to the inequality
({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}\leq 2[({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2}+({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}]
and the identity
\sum_{j,k}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k})^{2}={\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})
. ∎

C.5
Proof of Theorem
3

Proof of Part (i):

We have that,

{\rm E}(D_{\rm GWASH})={\rm E}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{n}{m}\hat{\ell}_{j,R}\right)=\frac{n}{m}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}\to\frac{\mu_{2}}{\lambda}.

Hence, in order to show convergence in
{\cal L}_{2}
we need to prove that
{\rm Var}(D_{\rm GWASH})\to 0
. By Lemma
4
below, under M
1
,

{\rm Var}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\ell}_{j}\right)\leq{C}\left(\frac{2}{nm^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}+\frac{1}{m^{2}n^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right).

(35)

Under
WD
1
,
\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})
goes to zero, and therefore also
\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})
; see (
33
). Further, by (
14
),
|\underline{\ell}_{j}-\ell_{j}|\leq C
for some
C
and hence the assumption (under
WD
1
) that
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}
is bounded implies that so is
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\ell}_{j}=\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})
. Therefore,

\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\leq\left(\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k})^{2}\right)^{1/2}=\left(\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right)^{1/2}\to 0.

Hence the bound of the variance in (
35
) goes to zero. This completes the proof of Part (i).

Proof of Part (ii):
The proof is similar to the one of Part (ii) of Theorem
2
, with the exception that one needs to show that
\frac{1}{m^{2}}E[{\rm tr}(\boldsymbol{S}^{4})]\to 0
; this is done in Lemma
5
below. ∎

Lemma 4
.

Suppose that M
1
holds, then for a constant
C

{\rm Var}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\ell}_{j}\right)\leq{C}\left(\frac{2}{nm^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}+\frac{1}{m^{2}n^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right).

Proof of Lemma
4
:

We have (by Taylor expansion or just simple algebra) that

(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p})^{2}=({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})^{2}+2{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})+(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})^{2}.

(36)

Recall that
\hat{\ell}_{j}=\sum_{p}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p})^{2}
.
Therefore,

{\rm Cov}(\hat{\ell}_{j},\hat{\ell}_{k})=4\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})+2\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2})\\
+2\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}{\rm Cov}((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2},\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})+\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\rm Cov}((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2}).

We shall now bound the above covariances using Condition M
1
(b). Recall that this condition requires that

|{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{2}})|\leq C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})
for some
C>0
and almost all
j,k,p_{1},p_{2}
, in the sense that the number of quadruples
(j,k,p_{1},p_{2})
for which the condition may be violated is of order
o(m^{3})
. We first assume that the inequality holds for all
j,k,p_{1},p_{2}
and then deal with the case where
o(m^{3})
violations are allowed. Condition M
1
(b) implies that

|{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})|=\left|\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}}{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{2}})\right|\leq C\frac{\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}}{n}.

(37)

We shall use the notation
{\cal X}_{i,j,p}:={\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p}-\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p}
. Notice that
E({\cal X}_{i,j,p})=0
. Under this notation

\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\cal X}_{i,j,p_{1}}.

Thus,

{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2})={\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2})\\
=\frac{1}{n^{3}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}Cov({\cal X}_{i_{1},j,p_{1}},{\cal X}_{i_{2},k,p_{2}}{\cal X}_{i_{3},k,p_{2}})=\frac{1}{n^{3}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}E({\cal X}_{i_{1},j,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i_{2},k,p_{2}}{\cal X}_{i_{3},k,p_{2}}).

The expectation is different from zero only when
i_{1}=i_{2}=i_{3}
and therefore,

{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2})\leq C/n^{2}.

(38)

The last computation is

{\rm Cov}((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2})=\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}{\rm Cov}({\cal X}_{i_{1},j,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i_{2},j,p_{1}},{\cal X}_{i_{3},k,p_{2}}{\cal X}_{i_{4},k,p_{2}}).

The covariance is different from zero in three cases: (1)
i_{1}=i_{3},i_{2}=i_{4}
;
(2)
i_{1}=i_{4},i_{2}=i_{3}
; and (3)
i_{1}=i_{2}=i_{3}=i_{4}
. Cases (1) and (2) are symmetric and the covariance is

{\rm Cov}({\cal X}_{i_{1},j,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i_{2},j,p_{1}},{\cal X}_{i_{1},k,p_{2}}{\cal X}_{i_{2},k,p_{2}})=\left\{{\rm E}({\cal X}_{i_{1},j,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i_{1},k,p_{2}})\right\}^{2}\leq C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2},

where the last inequality is due to M
1
(b). For Case (3) we bound the covariance by a constant. It follows that

{\rm Cov}((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2})\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}\leq\frac{2}{n^{2}}C[(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k})^{2}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}^{2}],

(39)

where the last inequality is true because for every
a,b
, we have that
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}\leq 1
.

Thus, (
37
), (
38
) and (
39
) imply that

{\rm Var}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\ell}_{j}\right)=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}{\rm Cov}(\hat{\ell}_{j},\hat{\ell}_{k})=\frac{4}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k,p_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})\\
+\frac{4}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k,p_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\rm Cov}[\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2}]+\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k,p_{1},p_{2}}{\rm Cov}[(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2}]\\
\leq\frac{C}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k,p_{1},p_{2}}\left(\frac{4}{n}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})+\frac{4}{n^{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}[(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k})^{2}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}^{2}]\right)\\
={4C}\left(\frac{2}{nm^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}+\frac{1}{m^{2}n^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right).

(40)

We now consider the case where violations of the inequality
|{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{2}})|\leq C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})
are allowed for a small number of quadruples
(j,k,p_{1},p_{2})
. Specifically, let
{\cal I}
be the set of quadruples
(j,k,p_{1},p_{2})
where the inequality is violated.
By assumption,
|{\cal I}|=o(m^{3})
.
For
(j,k,p_{1},p_{2})\in{\cal I}
we have instead of (
37
) that

|{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})|\leq\frac{C}{n}.

and instead of (
39
) we have

{\rm Cov}[(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2},(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})^{2}]\leq\frac{2C}{n^{2}}.

It is easy to verify that since
|{\cal I}|=o(m^{3})
, this changes the inequality in (
40
) by a term that is
o(1)
; hence, the same proof applies.
∎

Lemma 5
.

If M
1
and
WD
1
hold, then
\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm E}[{\rm tr}(\boldsymbol{S}^{4})]\to 0
.

Proof of Lemma
5
:

We have

{\rm E}({\rm tr}(\boldsymbol{S}^{4}))=\sum_{j,k}{\rm E}[(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,k}^{2})^{2}]=\sum_{j,k}\{[{\rm E}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,k}^{2})]^{2}+{\rm Var}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,k}^{2})\}.

Now,

{\rm E}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,k}^{2})=\sum_{p}E(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p}\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p})=\sum_{p}\{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p}+Cov(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p},\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p})\}\leq{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{j,k}+\frac{C}{n}\sum_{p}\{\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p}\}\\
={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{j,k}+C\frac{m}{n}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}+\frac{1}{n}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{j,k},

where the inequality is due to (
37
). Since
(a+b+c)^{2}\leq 3a^{2}+3b^{2}+3c^{2}
,

\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}[{\rm E}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,k}^{2})]^{2}\leq\frac{3}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{j,k})^{2}+\frac{3C}{nm}\sum_{j,k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k})^{2}+\frac{3}{m^{2}n}\sum_{j,k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{j,k})^{2}.

Now,
\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{j,k})^{2}=\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})\leq\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})
and the latter term converges to zero under
WD
1
. Also,

\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k})^{2}=\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})
converges to zero because
\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})\to 0
. It follows that
\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}[{\rm E}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,k}^{2})]^{2}\to 0
.

Let us now compute the variance

{\rm Var}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,k}^{2})={\rm Var}\left(\sum_{p}\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p}\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p}\right)=\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}).

In order to compute the above variance consider a similar expansion as in (
36
)

\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p}\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p}=(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p})+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p}(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p})+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p}\\
=(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p})+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p}\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p}\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p}.

(41)

Therefore,

{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})={\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}),(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})\right)\\
+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}),\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}\right)+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}{\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}),\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}\right)\\
+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}),\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}\right)+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}{\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}),\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}\right)\\
+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}})\\
+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}).

We now bound the above covariances. As in the proof of Lemma
3
we first assume that the inequality in M
1
(b) holds for all
j,k,p_{1},p_{2}
and then deal with the case where
o(m^{3})
violations are allowed. We have that

{\rm Cov}\left[(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}),(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})\right]\\
=\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}{\rm Cov}({\cal X}_{i_{1},j,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i_{2},k,p_{1}},{\cal X}_{i_{3},j,p_{2}}{\cal X}_{i_{4},k,p_{2}}).

The covariance is different from zero in three cases: (1)
i_{1}=i_{3},i_{2}=i_{4}
; (2)
i_{1}=i_{4},i_{2}=i_{3}
; and (3)
i_{1}=i_{2}=i_{3}=i_{4}
. The covariance in Case (1) is

{\rm Cov}({\cal X}_{i_{1},j,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i_{2},k,p_{1}},{\cal X}_{i_{1},j,p_{2}}{\cal X}_{i_{2},k,p_{2}})={\rm E}({\cal X}_{i,j,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i,k,p_{1}})E({\cal X}_{i,k,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i,k,p_{2}})\\
={\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{2}}){\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{2}})\\
\leq C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}),

where the inequality is due to the condition M
1
(b). For case (2) the covariance is

{\rm Cov}({\cal X}_{i_{1},j,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i_{2},k,p_{1}},{\cal X}_{i_{2},j,p_{2}}{\cal X}_{i_{1},k,p_{2}})={\rm E}({\cal X}_{i,j,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i,k,p_{2}}){\rm E}({\cal X}_{i,k,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i,j,p_{2}})\\
={\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{2}}){\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p_{2}})\leq C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}.

For Case (3) we bound the covariance by a constant. To sum up,

{\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}),(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})\right)\\
\leq\frac{C}{n^{2}}\left[(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})+(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}\right]+O(1/n^{3}).

(42)

We now compute

{\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}),\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}\right)={\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}),\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{n^{3}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}{\rm Cov}({\cal X}_{i_{1},j,p_{1}}{\cal X}_{i_{2},k,p_{1}},{\cal X}_{i_{3},k,p_{2}}).

The covariance is different from zero only when
i_{1}=i_{2}=i_{3}
and therefore, by the condition
M_{1}
(a),

{\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}),\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}\right)\leq C/n^{2}.

Finally, as in (
37
)

|{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})|=\left|\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}}{\rm Cov}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},k}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{2}})\right|\leq C\frac{\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}}{n}.

(43)

To sum up,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}{\rm Var}(\boldsymbol{S}^{2}_{j,k})=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k,p_{1},p_{2}}{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{m^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{j,k,p_{1},p_{2}}\left[(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})+(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})^{2}\right]

(44)

\displaystyle+O(1/m)+\frac{C}{m^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{j,k,p_{1},p_{2}}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})

(45)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{m^{2}n}\sum_{j,k,p_{1},p_{2}}\left[\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}})\right]

(46)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{m^{2}n}\sum_{j,k,p_{1},p_{2}}\left[\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})\right].

(47)

The expression in (
44
) is bounded by (using
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
and the fact that
\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\leq 1
)

\frac{5C}{n^{2}}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})+\frac{2C}{mn^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{3})+\frac{C}{m^{2}n^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4}),

which goes to zero under
WD
1
because
\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})\to 0
implies that
\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{3})\to 0
.
The expression in (
45
) is equal to
\frac{4C}{n^{2}}\sum_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}
, which can be bounded as follows

\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k}\leq\left(\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,k})^{2}\right)^{1/2}=\left(\frac{1}{m^{2}}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right)^{1/2}.

The expressions in (
46
) and (
47
) are equal to

\frac{2C}{nm}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{3})+\frac{6C}{m^{2}n}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4}),

which goes to zero. This completes the proof of the lemma if M
1
(b) holds for all
j,k,p_{1},p_{2}
.

When violations of M
1
(b) are allowed for quadruples
(j,k,p_{1},p_{2})
in a set
{\cal I}
, then for
(j,k,p_{1},p_{2})\in{\cal I}
instead of (
42
) we have

{\rm Cov}\left((\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{1}}),(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})(\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{k,p_{2}})\right)\leq\frac{C}{n^{2}},

and instead of (
43
) we have

|{\rm Cov}(\boldsymbol{S}_{j,p_{1}},\boldsymbol{S}_{k,p_{2}})|\leq\frac{C}{n}.

Since
|{\cal I}|=o(m^{3})
, this changes the inequality in (
44
) by a term that is
o(1)
and hence the same proof applies.
∎

C.6
Proof of Theorem
4

Theorem
4
is a special case of Theorem
5
because if
\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}\sim N({\boldsymbol{0}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
then
M_{2}
is satisfied, and therefore we will only prove Theorem
5
.

C.7
Proof of Theorem
5

Proof of Part (i):
Recall that
D_{\rm LDSC}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{n}{m}\hat{\ell}_{j,R}^{2}
.
Define

\hat{Z}_{j,R}:=\hat{\ell}_{j,R}-{\rm E}\left(\hat{\ell}_{j,R}\right)=\hat{\ell}_{j,R}-\underline{\ell}_{j}.

(48)

Hence,
\hat{\ell}_{j,R}=\underline{\ell}_{j}+\hat{Z}_{j,R}
. Therefore,

D_{\rm LDSC}=\frac{n}{m}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\underline{\ell}_{j}+\hat{Z}_{j,R}\right)^{2}=\frac{n}{m}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}^{2}+\frac{n}{m}\frac{2}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}\hat{Z}_{j,R}+\frac{n}{m}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{Z}_{j,R}^{2}.

(49)

The first summand in the right-hand side of (
49
) converges to
\underline{\mu}_{2}^{*}/\lambda
. The second summand converges to zero in
{\cal L}_{2}
because

{\rm E}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}\hat{Z}_{j,R}\right]^{2}\leq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}^{2}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\rm E}\left(\hat{Z}_{j,R}^{2}\right),

and
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}^{2}
is bounded and by Lemma
6
(i) below

{\rm E}\left(\hat{Z}_{j,R}^{2}\right)\leq C\left[\frac{1}{n}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}+2{\ell}_{j}^{2})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right]+O(1/n).

Under
WD
2
,
\ell_{j}^{2}
and
\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})
are bounded. For
\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}
notice that under
WD
2
,
\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})
is bounded and therefore, if we let
\mathaccentV{bar}016{\lambda}_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mathaccentV{bar}016{\lambda}_{m}
to be the eigenvalues of
\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, then

C\geq\frac{1}{m}{\rm tr}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{4})=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathaccentV{bar}016{\lambda}_{j}^{4}\geq\frac{1}{m}\mathaccentV{bar}016{\lambda}_{1}^{4}.

Hence,
\mathaccentV{bar}016{\lambda}_{1}\leq Cm^{1/4}
. Therefore,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}\leq\mathaccentV{bar}016{\lambda}_{1}^{3}\leq Cm^{3/4}
and therefore,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}/m\leq C/m^{1/4}
. It follows that
{\rm E}\left(\hat{Z}_{j,R}^{2}\right)\leq C/m^{1/4}
and therefore the second summand in the right-hand side of (
49
) converges to zero in
{\cal L}_{2}
.

By Lemma
6
(ii),

{\rm E}\left(\hat{Z}_{j,R}^{4}\right)\leq\frac{C}{n^{3}}\left(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{4}+4\frac{m}{\sqrt{n}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}+4\frac{m^{2}}{n}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{j,j}\right)+O(1/n).

As before, we have that
\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{4}\leq m
,
\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}\leq Cm^{3/4}
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{j,j}=\ell_{j}\leq C
. Therefore,
{\rm E}\left(\hat{Z}_{j,R}^{4}\right)\leq O(1/n)
, where
O(1/n)
does not depend on
j
.
It follows that the third summand in the right-hand side of (
49
) converges to zero in
{\cal L}_{2}
. This completes the proof of Part (i).

Proof of Part (ii):
Recall that
N_{\rm LDSC}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\ell}_{j,R}\left(u_{j}^{2}-1\right)
. Using the definition of
\hat{Z}_{j,R}
in (
48
), we can write

N_{\rm LDSC}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\underline{\ell}_{j}+\hat{Z}_{j,R})\left(u_{j}^{2}-1\right)=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}\left(u_{j}^{2}-1\right)+\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{Z}_{j,R}\left(u_{j}^{2}-1\right).

We next show that

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}\left(u_{j}^{2}-1\right)\to h^{2}\underline{\mu}_{2}^{*}/\lambda\text{ in }{\cal L}_{2}\text{ as }n,m\to\infty,

(50)

and that

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{Z}_{j,R}\left(u_{j}^{2}-1\right)\to 0\text{ in }{\cal L}_{2}\text{ as }n,m\to\infty,

(51)

which together imply that
N_{\rm LDSC}\to h^{2}\underline{\mu}_{2}^{*}/\lambda
in
{\cal L}_{2}
as
n,m\to\infty
.

Indeed, (
50
) is true because

{\rm E}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}\left({u}_{j}^{2}-1\right)\right]=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}\left({\rm E}[{\rm E}({u}_{j}^{2}|{\boldsymbol{X}})]-1\right)\\
=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}\left({\rm E}\left[h^{2}\left(\frac{n}{m}\hat{\ell}_{j}-d_{j}^{2}\right)+d_{j}^{2}\right]-1\right)=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}h^{2}{\rm E}\left(\frac{n}{m}\hat{\ell}_{j}-1\right)\\
=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}h^{2}\frac{n}{m}{\rm E}\left(\hat{\ell}_{j}-\frac{m}{n}\right)=h^{2}\frac{n}{m}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}^{2}\to h^{2}\underline{\mu}_{2}^{*}/\lambda,

where the first and second equality in the second line are due to (
6
) and the fact that
E(d_{j}^{2})=1
, and the second equality in the third line is due to the definition of
\underline{\ell}_{j}
in (
13
).
Also,

{\rm Var}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\underline{\ell}_{j}\left({u}_{j}^{2}-1\right)\right]=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}\underline{\ell}_{j}\underline{\ell}_{k}{\rm Cov}(u_{j}^{2},u_{k}^{2})\\
\leq C\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j,k}{\rm Cov}(u_{j}^{2},u_{k}^{2})=C{\rm Var}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(u_{j}^{2}-1)\right),

where the inequality is true because
{\ell}_{j}
is bounded under
WD
2
and hence also
\underline{\ell}_{j}
(recall (
14
)). It was shown in Theorem
3
(ii) that
{\rm Var}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(u_{j}^{2}-1)\right)
converges to zero under BKE, M
2
and
WD
1
(which is weaker than
WD
2
). Thus, the proof of (
50
) is completed.

We now show (
51
). We have that

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{Z}_{j,R}\left(u_{j}^{2}-1\right)=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{Z}_{j,R}u_{j}^{2}-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{Z}_{j,R}.

It was proved in Part (i) that
{\rm E}\left(\hat{Z}_{j,R}^{2}\right)\leq\frac{C}{m^{1/4}}
and hence
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{Z}_{j,R}
converges to zero in
{\cal L}_{2}
. Furthermore, recall that
\{\hat{Z}_{j,R}\}_{j=1}^{m}
is independent of
\{u_{j}^{2}\}_{j=1}^{m}
. Therefore,

{\rm E}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{Z}_{j,R}u_{j}^{2}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}}{\rm E}\left(\hat{Z}_{j_{1},R}\hat{Z}_{j_{2},R}\right){\rm E}\left(u_{j_{1}}^{2}u_{j_{2}}^{2}\right)\\
\leq\frac{C}{m^{1/4}}\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}}{\rm E}\left(u_{j_{1}}^{2}u_{j_{2}}^{2}\right)=\frac{C}{m^{1/4}}{\rm E}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}u_{j}^{2}\right)^{2}.

It was shown in Theorem
3
(ii) that
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(u_{j}^{2}-1)
converges to a finite limit in
{\cal L}_{2}
and hence
{\rm E}\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}u_{j}^{2}\right)^{2}
is bounded. Therefore, (
51
) follows. Thus, the proof of
Part (ii) is completed. ∎

Lemma 6
.

Suppose that M
2
holds, and that
m/n\to\lambda>0
. Let
\hat{Z}_{j,R}
as defined in (
48
), then for a constant
C
,

1.

{\rm E}\left(\hat{Z}_{j,R}^{2}\right)\leq C\left[\frac{1}{n}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}+2{\ell}_{j}^{2})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right]+O(1/n)
;

2.

{\rm E}\left(\hat{Z}_{j,R}^{4}\right)\leq\frac{C}{n^{3}}\left(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{4}+4\frac{m}{\sqrt{n}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}+4\frac{m^{2}}{n}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{j,j}\right)+O(1/n)
,

where the
O
terms are uniform in
j
.

Proof of Part (i):

The definitions of
\hat{\ell}_{j,R}
in (
7
) implies that
\hat{\ell}_{j,R}
has the same distribution as
\hat{\ell}_{j}-\frac{m}{n}
. Therefore,
\hat{Z}_{j,R}
is distributed like
\hat{\ell}_{j}-\frac{m}{n}-\underline{\ell}_{j}
.
We continue with this term, recalling the computation of
\underline{\ell}_{j}
in
(
14
),

\displaystyle\hat{\ell}_{j}-\frac{m}{n}-\underline{\ell}_{j}

\displaystyle=\hat{\ell}_{j}-\frac{m}{n}-\ell_{j}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{p=1}^{m}\left[{\rm Var}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p})-1\right]

\displaystyle=\hat{\ell}_{j}-\ell_{j}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{p=1}^{m}{\rm Var}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{m}\left[{\boldsymbol{S}}_{jp}^{2}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})^{2}-\frac{{\rm Var}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p})}{n}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{m}\left[\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{1},j}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{1},p}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{2},j}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{2},p}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})^{2}-\frac{{\rm Var}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p})}{n}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{m}\left[\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}}\left({{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{1},j}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{1},p}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{2},j}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{2},p}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})^{2}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\quad+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p}^{2}-[({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})^{2}+{\rm Var}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p})]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{m}V_{j,p}+\sum_{p=1}^{m}W_{j,p},

where

V_{j,p}:=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}}\left({{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{1},j}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{1},p}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{2},j}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{2},p}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})^{2}\right)\\
\text{ and }W_{j,p}:=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p}^{2}-[({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})^{2}+{\rm Var}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p})]\right\}.

Notice that for
i_{1}\neq i_{2}

{\rm E}\left({{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{1},j}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{1},p}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{2},j}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{2},p}\right)=({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})^{2}

and

{\rm E}\left({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p}^{2}\right)=\left[{\rm E}\left({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p}\right)\right]^{2}+{\rm Var}\left({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p}\right)=({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p})^{2}+{\rm Var}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i,p}).

Hence,
{\rm E}(W_{j,p})={\rm E}(V_{j,p})=0
.
We want to compute
E\left(\sum_{p=1}^{m}V_{j,p}\right)^{2}
and
{\rm E}\left(\sum_{p=1}^{m}W_{j,p}\right)^{2}
.
To this end, we compute

{\rm E}(V_{j,p_{1}}V_{j,p_{2}})\\
=\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}}\sum_{i_{3}\neq i_{4}}{\rm E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}][{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{3},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{3},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}].

The expectation is different from zero if (a) one of
i_{1},i_{2}
is equal to one of
i_{3},i_{4}
and (b) when the set
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
is equal to the set
\{i_{3},i_{4}\}
. Consider now case (a). If
i_{1}=i_{3}
and
i_{2}\neq i_{4}
, then

{\rm E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}][{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}]\\
={\rm E}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}\\
\leq C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+2\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}\\
\leq C\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}+2C\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}^{2}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}^{2},

where we used the inequality in M
2
(b). Notice that the set of indices
j,p_{1},p_{2}
where the inequality does not hold (which is of order
O(m^{3})
) is negligible because we divide by
n^{4}
. Therefore, we assume without loss of generality that the inequality holds for all
j,p_{1},p_{2}
. Case (a) occurs for
4n(n-1)(n-2)
indices of
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}
.

Consider now case (b). Then,

{\rm E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}][{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2,j}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}]\\
=\{{\rm E}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}})\}^{2}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}\leq C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}+2\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}\\
\leq C\left(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}^{2}+4\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}^{2}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}^{2}+4\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}\right),

where we again use the inequality in M
2
(b) and assume without loss of generality that the inequality holds for all
j,p_{1},p_{2}
. Case (b) occurs for
2n(n-1)
indices of
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}
.

It follows that

\displaystyle{\rm E}\left(\sum_{p=1}^{m}V_{j,p}\right)^{2}=\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\rm E}(V_{j,p_{1}}V_{j,p_{2}})\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{4(n-1)(n-2)}{n^{3}}\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}C[\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}+2\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}^{2}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}^{2}]

\displaystyle+\frac{2(n-1)}{n^{3}}\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}^{2}+4\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}}^{2}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}^{2}+4\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{p_{1},p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{4(n-1)(n-2)}{n^{3}}C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}+2{\ell}_{j}^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{2(n-1)}{n^{3}}C({\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})+4{\ell}_{j}^{2}+4\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{4+O(1/n)}{n}C(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}+2{\ell}_{j}^{2})+\frac{2}{n^{2}}C{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})+O(1/n^{2}).

To compute
{\rm E}(W_{j,p_{1}}W_{j,p_{2}})
notice that

W_{j,p_{1}}W_{j,p_{2}}=\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}}\left\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}^{2}-[({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}+{\rm Var}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}})]\right\}\\
\left\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{2}}^{2}-[({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}+{\rm Var}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{2}})]\right\},

and that the expectation is different from zero only when
i_{1}=i_{2}
. Since the moments are bounded,
{\rm E}(W_{j,p_{1}}W_{j,p_{2}})=O(1/n^{3})
.
Thus,

{\rm E}\left(\sum_{p=1}^{m}W_{j,p}\right)^{2}=\sum_{p_{1},p_{2}}{\rm E}(W_{j,p_{1}}W_{j,p_{2}})=O(1/n).

It follows that

{\rm E}\left(\hat{Z}_{j,R}^{2}\right)\leq{2}{\rm E}\left(\sum_{p=1}^{m}V_{j,p}\right)^{2}+{2}{\rm E}\left(\sum_{p=1}^{m}W_{j,p}\right)^{2}\\
\leq C\left(\frac{8+O(1/n)}{n}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,j}^{3}+2{\ell}_{j}^{2})+\frac{4}{n^{2}}{\rm tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\right)+O(1/n),

which completes the proof of Part (i).

Proof of Part (ii):

We start with bounding
{\rm E}\left(\sum_{p=1}^{m}V_{j,p}\right)^{4}
. Compute

{\rm E}(V^{2}_{j,p_{1}}V^{2}_{j,p_{2}})=\frac{1}{n^{8}}\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}}\sum_{i_{3}\neq i_{4}}\sum_{i_{5}\neq i_{6}}\sum_{i_{7}\neq i_{8}}{\rm E}\Big\{[{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{1},j}{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}]\\
[{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{3},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{3},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{1}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}][{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{5},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{5},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{6},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{6},p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}]\\
[{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{7},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{7},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{8},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{8},p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}]\Big\}.

The expectation is not zero if at least one index appears in all the pairs
(i_{1},i_{2}),(i_{3},i_{4}),(i_{5},i_{6}),(i_{7},i_{8})
. Suppose that
i_{1}=i_{3}=i_{5}=i_{7}
, then we need to compute

{\rm E}\Big\{[{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2})][{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{1}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}]\\
[{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{6},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{6},p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}][{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{8},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{8},p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}]\Big\}.

This sum is composed of 16 terms, all of which can be bounded by
C({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}
when the moments are bounded. For example,

{\rm E}\Big({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}^{4}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{4},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{6},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{6},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{8},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{8},p_{2}}\Big)\\
={\rm E}\Big({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}^{4}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}^{2}\Big)({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}.

This case and symmetric ones occur for
O(n^{5})
indices of
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5},i_{6},i_{7},i_{8}
,
It follows that under the case where
i_{1}=i_{3}=i_{5}=i_{7}
(and symmetric cases) the expectation can be bounded by
\frac{C}{n^{3}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}
.

Another case to consider is where one index appears in all the pairs
(i_{1},i_{2}),(i_{3},i_{4}),(i_{5},i_{6}),(i_{7},i_{8})
and another index appears in another pair, e.g.,
i_{1}=i_{3}=i_{5}=i_{7}
and
i_{2}=i_{4}
. Then we need to compute

{\rm E}\Big\{[{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}][{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{1}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{2},p_{1}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}]\\
[{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{6},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{6},p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}][{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{1},p_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{8},j}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i_{8},p_{2}}-({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}]\Big\}

In this case, all the summands can be bounded by
C({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}
. This case and symmetric ones occur for
O(n^{4})
indices of
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5},i_{6},i_{7},i_{8}
.

Thus, to sum up,

{\rm E}(V^{2}_{j,p_{1}}V^{2}_{j,p_{2}})\leq\frac{C}{n^{3}}\left(({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}+({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}/n+({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}/n\right)+O(1/n^{5}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle{\rm E}\left(\sum_{p=1}^{m}V_{j,p}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}}{\rm E}(V_{j,p_{1}}V_{j,p_{2}}V_{j,p_{3}}V_{j,p_{4}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}}\left\{{\rm E}\left(V_{j,p_{1}}^{2}V_{j,p_{2}}^{2}\right){\rm E}\left(V^{2}_{j,p_{3}}V^{2}_{j,p_{4}}\right)\right\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n^{3}}\sum_{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}}\left\{({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}+\frac{({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{1}})^{2}}{n}+\frac{({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{j,p_{2}})^{2}}{n}\right\}^{1/2}
